### Chapter 4.5 — Transformer Networks

#### Learning objectives
*   Explain the concept of the attention mechanism and its significance in sequence modeling.
*   Describe the architecture of the Transformer model, including its encoder and decoder components.
*   Understand the function of multi-head self-attention and positional encoding within Transformers.
*   Identify the key advantages of Transformers over traditional RNNs and CNNs for sequence-to-sequence tasks.
*   Understand the basic principles behind large language models (LLMs) like BERT and GPT, which are based on the Transformer architecture.

#### Detailed lesson content
While LSTMs and GRUs significantly improved upon traditional RNNs by addressing the vanishing gradient problem and learning long-term dependencies, they still suffer from a fundamental limitation: their sequential nature. Processing sequences step-by-step means that computations cannot be fully parallelized, which becomes a bottleneck for very long sequences and large datasets. This is where **Transformer Networks**, introduced in the groundbreaking 2017 paper "Attention Is All You Need," revolutionized sequence modeling. Transformers completely discard recurrence and convolutions, relying entirely on an **attention mechanism** to draw global dependencies between input and output.

At the heart of the Transformer is the **attention mechanism**, particularly **self-attention**. Attention allows the model to weigh the importance of different parts of the input sequence when processing each element. Instead of processing a word based only on its immediate predecessors (like an RNN) or a fixed local window (like a CNN), self-attention enables a word to "look at" and derive context from all other words in the input sequence, regardless of their position. For example, in the sentence "The animal didn't cross the street because it was too tired," to understand what "it" refers to, a model needs to attend to "animal." Self-attention provides a mechanism for this.

The Transformer architecture is typically composed of an **encoder** and a **decoder**, both consisting of multiple identical layers.
*   **Encoder:** The encoder stack processes the input sequence. Each encoder layer has two sub-layers: a **multi-head self-attention mechanism** and a simple, position-wise fully connected feed-forward network. A residual connection and layer normalization are applied around each of these sub-layers.
*   **Decoder:** The decoder stack generates the output sequence. Each decoder layer has three sub-layers: a masked multi-head self-attention mechanism (to prevent attending to future tokens), a multi-head attention mechanism that attends to the output of the encoder stack, and a position-wise fully connected feed-forward network.

Let's delve deeper into some key components:
*   **Multi-Head Self-Attention:** Instead of performing a single attention function, multi-head attention linearly projects the queries, keys, and values `h` times with different, learned linear projections. These `h` parallel attention layers then concatenate their outputs and project them once more. This allows the model to jointly attend to information from different representation subspaces at different positions. It's like having multiple "lenses" to look at the same context.
*   **Positional Encoding:** Since the Transformer architecture completely removes recurrence and convolution, it inherently loses information about the order of the sequence. To reintroduce this crucial positional information, **positional encodings** are added to the input embeddings at the bottom of the encoder and decoder stacks. These are vectors that carry information about the relative or absolute position of tokens in the sequence. They have the same dimension as the embeddings, so the two can be summed. The positional encodings are often learned or generated using fixed sine and cosine functions of different frequencies.
*   **Feed-Forward Networks:** Each encoder and decoder layer contains a simple, fully connected feed-forward network, which is applied independently to each position. This network consists of two linear transformations with a ReLU activation in between.
*   **Residual Connections and Layer Normalization:** These are crucial for training deep networks. Residual connections (also known as skip connections) help mitigate the vanishing gradient problem by allowing gradients to bypass layers. Layer normalization normalizes the inputs across the features, stabilizing training.

**Advantages of Transformers:**
1.  **Parallelization:** The absence of recurrent connections allows for parallel computation across all time steps, significantly speeding up training, especially on GPUs.
2.  **Long-Range Dependencies:** The self-attention mechanism can directly capture dependencies between any two words in a sequence, regardless of their distance, overcoming the limitations of RNNs which struggle with very long-term dependencies.
3.  **Transfer Learning:** Transformers have proven exceptionally good for pre-training on massive datasets and then fine-tuning for specific downstream tasks, leading to the development of powerful Large Language Models (LLMs) like BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers) and GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer). BERT uses an encoder-only architecture for understanding context, while GPT uses a decoder-only architecture for text generation.

A common mistake when first encountering Transformers is to assume they inherently understand order. Without positional encoding, a Transformer would treat a bag of words as input, losing all sequential information. Another challenge is the quadratic computational complexity of self-attention with respect to sequence length, which can be a bottleneck for extremely long sequences.

```python
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.nn.functional as F
import math

# 1. Positional Encoding (conceptual implementation)
class PositionalEncoding(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, d_model, max_len=5000):
        super(PositionalEncoding, self).__init__()
        pe = torch.zeros(max_len, d_model)
        position = torch.arange(0, max_len, dtype=torch.float).unsqueeze(1)
        div_term = torch.exp(torch.arange(0, d_model, 2).float() * (-math.log(10000.0) / d_model))
        pe[:, 0::2] = torch.sin(position * div_term)
        pe[:, 1::2] = torch.cos(position * div_term)
        pe = pe.unsqueeze(0) # Add batch dimension
        self.register_buffer('pe', pe) # Register as buffer, not parameter

    def forward(self, x):
        # x is (batch_size, seq_len, d_model)
        # Add positional encoding to the input embeddings
        x = x + self.pe[:, :x.size(1), :]
        return x

# 2. Self-Attention Mechanism (simplified for understanding)
class SelfAttention(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, embed_dim, head_dim):
        super(SelfAttention, self).__init__()
        self.head_dim = head_dim
        self.query_proj = nn.Linear(embed_dim, head_dim)
        self.key_proj = nn.Linear(embed_dim, head_dim)
        self.value_proj = nn.Linear(embed_dim, head_dim)

    def forward(self, query, key, value, mask=None):
        # Project inputs
        Q = self.query_proj(query) # (batch_size, seq_len, head_dim)
        K = self.key_proj(key)     # (batch_size, seq_len, head_dim)
        V = self.value_proj(value) # (batch_size, seq_len, head_dim)

        # Calculate attention scores
        # (batch_size, seq_len, head_dim) @ (batch_size, head_dim, seq_len) -> (batch_size, seq_len, seq_len)
        scores = torch.matmul(Q, K.transpose(-2, -1)) / math.sqrt(self.head_dim)

        # Apply mask (for decoder, to prevent attending to future tokens)
        if mask is not None:
            scores = scores.masked_fill(mask == 0, float('-inf'))

        # Apply softmax to get attention probabilities
        attention_weights = F.softmax(scores, dim=-1) # (batch_size, seq_len, seq_len)

        # Multiply by values to get context vector
        output = torch.matmul(attention_weights, V) # (batch_size, seq_len, head_dim)
        return output, attention_weights

# 3. Multi-Head Attention (conceptual, combining multiple SelfAttention instances)
class MultiHeadAttention(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, embed_dim, num_heads):
        super(MultiHeadAttention, self).__init__()
        self.num_heads = num_heads
        self.head_dim = embed_dim // num_heads
        assert embed_dim % num_heads == 0, "embed_dim must be divisible by num_heads"

        self.heads = nn.ModuleList([
            SelfAttention(embed_dim, self.head_dim) for _ in range(num_heads)
        ])
        self.output_proj = nn.Linear(embed_dim, embed_dim) # Project concatenated heads back to embed_dim

    def forward(self, query, key, value, mask=None):
        # For simplicity, this is a conceptual multi-head.
        # In actual implementation, Q, K, V are usually split/reshaped
        # before being passed to attention calculation, then concatenated.
        
        # Here, we'll run each head independently and concatenate their outputs.
        # This is less efficient than the standard implementation but conceptually clear.
        head_outputs = []
        for head in self.heads:
            output, _ = head(query, key, value, mask)
            head_outputs.append(output)
        
        # Concatenate outputs from all heads
        concatenated_output = torch.cat(head_outputs, dim=-1) # (batch_size, seq_len, embed_dim)
        
        # Project back to original embedding dimension
        output = self.output_proj(concatenated_output)
        return output

# 4. A simplified Transformer Encoder Layer (conceptual)
class TransformerEncoderLayer(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, embed_dim, num_heads, ff_dim, dropout=0.1):
        super(TransformerEncoderLayer, self).__init__()
        self.multi_head_attention = MultiHeadAttention(embed_dim, num_heads)
        self.norm1 = nn.LayerNorm(embed_dim)
        self.dropout1 = nn.Dropout(dropout)

        self.feed_forward = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Linear(embed_dim, ff_dim),
            nn.ReLU(),
            nn.Linear(ff_dim, embed_dim)
        )
        self.norm2 = nn.LayerNorm(embed_dim)
        self.dropout2 = nn.Dropout(dropout)

    def forward(self, x, mask=None):
        # Multi-Head Self-Attention
        attn_output = self.multi_head_attention(x, x, x, mask) # Q, K, V are all x for self-attention
        x = self.norm1(x + self.dropout1(attn_output)) # Add & Norm

        # Feed Forward
        ff_output = self.feed_forward(x)
        x = self.norm2(x + self.dropout2(ff_output)) # Add & Norm
        return x

# Example usage (conceptual):
# embed_dim = 512
# num_heads = 8
# ff_dim = 2048
# vocab_size = 10000
# max_seq_len = 100
# batch_size = 2

# # Input embeddings + positional encoding
# word_embeddings = nn.Embedding(vocab_size, embed_dim)
# positional_encoding = PositionalEncoding(embed_dim, max_len=max_seq_len)

# input_tokens = torch.randint(0, vocab_size, (batch_size, max_seq_len))
# x = word_embeddings(input_tokens) # (batch_size, max_seq_len, embed_dim)
# x = positional_encoding(x)

# encoder_output = encoder_layer(x)

```
This code provides conceptual implementations of key Transformer components like Positional Encoding, Self-Attention, Multi-Head Attention, and a simplified Encoder Layer. It helps illustrate how these pieces fit together, though actual production-level Transformer implementations (e.g., `nn.TransformerEncoderLayer` in PyTorch) are highly optimized.

#### Key concepts
*   **Transformer Network:** A neural network architecture that relies entirely on attention mechanisms to process sequential data, discarding recurrence and convolutions.
*   **Attention Mechanism:** A technique that allows a model to weigh the importance of different parts of the input sequence when processing each element.
*   **Self-Attention:** An attention mechanism where the input sequence attends to itself, allowing each element to derive context from all other elements in the same sequence.
*   **Encoder:** The part of the Transformer that processes the input sequence and produces a contextualized representation.
*   **Decoder:** The part of the Transformer that takes the encoder's output and generates the target output sequence.
*   **Multi-Head Attention:** An extension of self-attention that performs multiple attention calculations in parallel, allowing the model to attend to different parts of the input from different representation subspaces.
*   **Positional Encoding:** Vectors added to input embeddings to provide information about the relative or absolute position of tokens in the sequence, as Transformers lack inherent sequential processing.
*   **Feed-Forward Network:** A simple, fully connected neural network applied independently to each position within an encoder or decoder layer.
*   **Residual Connections (Skip Connections):** Connections that add the input of a layer to its output, helping to train deeper networks by mitigating vanishing gradients.
*   **Layer Normalization:** A normalization technique applied across the features of an input, stabilizing training.
*   **BERT (Bidirectional Encoder Representations from Transformers):** A pre-trained language model based on the Transformer encoder, designed for understanding context.
*   **GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer):** A family of pre-trained language models based on the Transformer decoder, designed for text generation.

#### Hands-on activity
**Explore a Pre-trained Transformer Encoder (BERT-like)**

Your task is to load a pre-trained Transformer encoder model (like a small BERT variant) and use its tokenizer and model to generate embeddings for a sentence. This will give you a practical feel for how these models process text.

**Instructions:**
1.  Install the `transformers` library from Hugging Face (`pip install transformers`).
2.  Load a pre-trained tokenizer and model, for example, `bert-base-uncased`.
3.  Tokenize a sample sentence and convert it to input IDs and attention masks.
4.  Pass these inputs through the BERT model to obtain the hidden states (embeddings).
5.  Inspect the shape of the output embeddings.

```python
from transformers import AutoTokenizer, AutoModel
import torch

# 1. Load pre-trained tokenizer and model
# We'll use a small, common BERT model for demonstration
model_name = "bert-base-uncased"
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer.from_pretrained(model_name)
model = AutoModel.from_pretrained(model_name)

# Set model to evaluation mode
model.eval()

# 2. Define a sample sentence
sentence = "Transformers are revolutionizing natural language processing."

# 3. Tokenize the sentence and prepare inputs
# `return_tensors="pt"` returns PyTorch tensors
# `padding=True` pads to the longest sequence in the batch (or max_length if specified)
# `truncation=True` truncates if the sequence is longer than max_length
inputs = tokenizer(sentence, return_tensors="pt", padding=True, truncation=True)

print(f"Input IDs: {inputs['input_ids']}")
print(f"Attention Mask: {inputs['attention_mask']}")
print(f"Tokenized sentence: {tokenizer.convert_ids_to_tokens(inputs['input_ids'][0])}")

# 4. Pass inputs through the model to get embeddings
# No gradient calculation needed for inference
with torch.no_grad():
    outputs = model(**inputs)

# The `last_hidden_state` contains the contextualized embeddings for each token
last_hidden_states = outputs.last_hidden_state

print(f"\nShape of last hidden states (embeddings): {last_hidden_states.shape}")
# Expected: (batch_size, sequence_length, hidden_size)
# For bert-base-uncased, hidden_size is 768.

# You can also get the pooled output (often used for classification tasks)
# pooled_output = outputs.pooler_output
# print(f"Shape of pooled output: {pooled_output.shape}")
# Expected: (batch_size, hidden_size)

print("\nTask: Experiment with different sentences and observe the tokenization and output shapes.")
print("Consider what each dimension in the `last_hidden_state` represents.")

```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** What is the primary reason Transformers use positional encodings?
    *   A) To reduce the computational cost of the attention mechanism.
    *   B) To introduce non-linearity into the model's computations.
    *   C) To provide the model with information about the order of tokens in a sequence, as self-attention is permutation-invariant.
    *   D) To help mitigate the vanishing gradient problem in very deep Transformer models.

    **Correct Answer:** C) To provide the model with information about the order of tokens in a sequence, as self-attention is permutation-invariant.
    **Explanation:** Unlike RNNs, which process sequences sequentially and thus inherently capture order, the self-attention mechanism in Transformers computes relationships between all tokens simultaneously, making it permutation-invariant (meaning the output would be the same regardless of the input order if not for positional encodings). Positional encodings are explicitly added to the input embeddings to inject information about the relative or absolute position of each token, which is crucial for understanding sentence structure and meaning.

2.  **Question:** A researcher is working on a machine translation task and needs to process extremely long sentences (e.g., paragraphs). They are comparing an LSTM-based sequence-to-sequence model with a Transformer-based model. Which of the following is a significant advantage of the Transformer in this scenario?
    *   A) Transformers require less training data than LSTMs for long sequences.
    *   B) Transformers can process all tokens in a sequence in parallel, leading to faster training and better capture of long-range dependencies.
    *   C) LSTMs are more robust to exploding gradients in long sequences, making them easier to train.
    *   D) Transformers have a simpler architecture with fewer parameters, reducing the risk of overfitting.

    **Correct Answer:** B) Transformers can process all tokens in a sequence in parallel, leading to faster training and better capture of long-range dependencies.
    **Explanation:** For extremely long sequences, the Transformer's ability to process all tokens in parallel via self-attention is a major advantage over LSTMs, which process tokens sequentially. This parallelization significantly speeds up training. Furthermore, self-attention can directly model dependencies between any two tokens, regardless of their distance, making it highly effective at capturing long-range dependencies that LSTMs might struggle with, even with their gating mechanisms. Transformers generally have more parameters than LSTMs and are not inherently simpler (D), nor do they necessarily require less data (A). LSTMs are better at mitigating vanishing gradients than simple RNNs, but Transformers address the problem differently and often more effectively for very long sequences.

### Chapter 4.6 — Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs)

#### Learning objectives
*   Explain the core concept of Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs) and their adversarial training process.
*   Describe the architecture and role of the Generator and Discriminator networks within a GAN.
*   Understand the minimax game theory behind GAN training and its objective function.
*   Identify common challenges in training GANs, such as mode collapse and training instability.
*   Implement a basic GAN for generating synthetic data (e.g., images).

#### Detailed lesson content
So far, we've focused on discriminative models, which learn to map input data to labels (e.g., classifying an image as a cat or dog). Now, we shift our attention to **generative models**, which aim to learn the underlying distribution of the training data to generate new, similar data samples. Among generative models, **Generative Adversarial Networks (GANs)**, introduced by Ian Goodfellow et al. in 2014, have emerged as one of the most exciting and powerful techniques, capable of producing incredibly realistic images, audio, and even text.

The core idea behind a GAN is an **adversarial training process** involving two neural networks, a **Generator** and a **Discriminator**, locked in a zero-sum game. Think of it as a counterfeiter (the Generator) trying to produce fake currency that looks real, and a police detective (the Discriminator) trying to distinguish between real and fake currency.
*   **Generator (G):** This network takes a random noise vector (often sampled from a simple distribution like a Gaussian or uniform distribution) as input and transforms it into a data sample (e.g., an image). Its goal is to generate samples that are indistinguishable from real data.
*   **Discriminator (D):** This network takes a data sample (either a real sample from the training data or a fake sample generated by G) as input and outputs a probability that the sample is real. Its goal is to correctly classify real samples as real and fake samples as fake.

The two networks are trained simultaneously in a **minimax game**.
*   The **Generator** tries to minimize `log(1 - D(G(z)))`, where `G(z)` is the fake data generated from noise `z`. In simpler terms, it wants the Discriminator to output a high probability (close to 1) that its generated samples are real.
*   The **Discriminator** tries to maximize `log(D(x)) + log(1 - D(G(z)))`, where `x` is real data. It wants to output a high probability for real data (`D(x)` close to 1) and a low probability for fake data (`D(G(z))` close to 0).

During training, the Generator and Discriminator continuously improve by competing against each other. The Generator learns to produce more realistic data to fool the Discriminator, while the Discriminator learns to become better at detecting fakes. Ideally, this process continues until the Generator creates samples that are so realistic that the Discriminator can no longer distinguish them from real data (i.e., `D(x) = D(G(z)) = 0.5`).

The architectures of the Generator and Discriminator typically consist of deep neural networks. For image generation, the Generator often uses **transposed convolutional layers** (also known as deconvolutional layers or fractionally-strided convolutions) to upsample the noise vector into an image. The Discriminator typically uses standard **convolutional layers** to downsample the input image and classify it. Both often use ReLU activations in hidden layers, with Tanh in the Generator's output for images (to map pixel values to -1 to 1) and Sigmoid in the Discriminator's output for binary classification.

**Common Challenges in GAN Training:**
1.  **Training Instability:** GANs are notoriously difficult to train. The minimax game is a delicate balance, and if one network becomes too powerful too quickly, the other might fail to learn. For example, if the Discriminator becomes too good, the Generator's gradients might vanish, preventing it from learning.
2.  **Mode Collapse:** This occurs when the Generator learns to produce only a limited variety of samples, often focusing on a few types of samples that are particularly good at fooling the Discriminator, rather than learning the full diversity of the real data distribution. For example, a GAN trained on MNIST might only generate digit '1's.
3.  **Vanishing Gradients (for Generator):** If the Discriminator is too strong, `D(G(z))` might quickly go to zero, leading to `log(1 - D(G(z)))` becoming very large and the gradient for the Generator becoming very small. A common trick is to train the Generator to maximize `log(D(G(z)))` instead, which provides stronger gradients early in training.
4.  **No Objective Metric:** Evaluating GANs is challenging. There isn't a single loss value that directly tells you how good the generated samples are. Metrics like Inception Score (IS) or Frechet Inception Distance (FID) are used, but they require pre-trained image classification models and are not perfect.

Despite these challenges, GANs have found incredible applications:
*   **Realistic Image Generation:** Creating photorealistic faces, landscapes, and objects.
*   **Image-to-Image Translation:** Converting satellite images to maps, photos to paintings (e.g., CycleGAN).
*   **Data Augmentation:** Generating synthetic data to expand limited datasets for other machine learning tasks.
*   **Super-Resolution:** Enhancing the resolution of low-resolution images.
*   **Style Transfer:** Applying the artistic style of one image to another.

Safety note: When generating images, especially human faces, it's crucial to consider ethical implications regarding deepfakes, misinformation, and bias in training data that might lead to biased or harmful generated content. Always ensure responsible use and transparency.

```python
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.optim as optim
from torchvision import datasets, transforms
from torch.utils.data import DataLoader
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

# 1. Hyperparameters
latent_dim = 100 # Size of the noise vector
img_size = 28    # MNIST image size
num_channels = 1 # Grayscale MNIST
batch_size = 64
num_epochs = 50
lr = 0.0002

# 2. Data loading and preprocessing (MNIST)
transform = transforms.Compose([
    transforms.ToTensor(),
    transforms.Normalize((0.5,), (0.5,)) # Normalize to [-1, 1]
])

train_dataset = datasets.MNIST(root='./data', train=True, download=True, transform=transform)
train_loader = DataLoader(train_dataset, batch_size=batch_size, shuffle=True)

# 3. Define the Generator Network
class Generator(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, latent_dim, img_size, num_channels):
        super(Generator, self).__init__()
        self.img_size = img_size
        self.num_channels = num_channels

        # Start with a linear layer to project noise into a feature map
        self.fc = nn.Linear(latent_dim, 128 * (img_size // 4) * (img_size // 4)) # e.g., 128 * 7 * 7 for 28x28 img

        self.main = nn.Sequential(
            # Reshape to (batch_size, 128, img_size//4, img_size//4)
            nn.BatchNorm2d(128),
            nn.ReLU(True),
            # Transposed Conv 1: (128, 7, 7) -> (64, 14, 14)
            nn.ConvTranspose2d(128, 64, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),
            nn.BatchNorm2d(64),
            nn.ReLU(True),
            # Transposed Conv 2: (64, 14, 14) -> (num_channels, 28, 28)
            nn.ConvTranspose2d(64, num_channels, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),
            nn.Tanh() # Output pixel values between -1 and 1
        )

    def forward(self, input):
        x = self.fc(input)
        x = x.view(x.size(0), 128, self.img_size // 4, self.img_size // 4)
        return self.main(x)

# 4. Define the Discriminator Network
class Discriminator(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, img_size, num_channels):
        super(Discriminator, self).__init__()
        self.main = nn.Sequential(
            # Conv 1: (num_channels, 28, 28) -> (64, 14, 14)
            nn.Conv2d(num_channels, 64, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),
            nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, inplace=True),
            # Conv 2: (64, 14, 14) -> (128, 7, 7)
            nn.Conv2d(64, 128, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),
            nn.BatchNorm2d(128),
            nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, inplace=True),
            # Flatten and classify
            nn.Conv2d(128, 1, kernel_size=(img_size // 4), stride=1, padding=0), # Output 1x1 feature map
            nn.Sigmoid() # Output probability between 0 and 1
        )

    def forward(self, input):
        return self.main(input).view(-1, 1) # Flatten to (batch_size, 1)

# 5. Instantiate models, loss, and optimizers
device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
generator = Generator(latent_dim, img_size, num_channels).to(device)
discriminator = Discriminator(img_size, num_channels).to(device)

criterion = nn.BCELoss() # Binary Cross-Entropy Loss for binary classification

optimizer_G = optim.Adam(generator.parameters(), lr=lr, betas=(0.5, 0.999))
optimizer_D = optim.Adam(discriminator.parameters(), lr=lr, betas=(0.5, 0.999))

# Fixed noise for visualization of generator's progress
fixed_noise = torch.randn(64, latent_dim, device=device)

# 6. Training Loop
print("Starting GAN Training...")
for epoch in range(num_epochs):
    for i, (real_images, _) in enumerate(train_loader):
        real_images = real_images.to(device)
        b_size = real_images.size(0)

        # Create labels for real and fake data
        real_labels = torch.full((b_size, 1), 1.0, dtype=torch.float, device=device)
        fake_labels = torch.full((b_size, 1), 0.0, dtype=torch.float, device=device)

        ##############################
        # (1) Update Discriminator network: maximize log(D(x)) + log(1 - D(G(z)))
        ##############################
        discriminator.zero_grad()

        # Train with real images
        output_real = discriminator(real_images)
        errD_real = criterion(output_real, real_labels)
        errD_real.backward()

        # Train with fake images
        noise = torch.randn(b_size, latent_dim, device=device)
        fake_images = generator(noise) # Generate fake images
        output_fake = discriminator(fake_images.detach()) # Detach to prevent G from updating
        errD_fake = criterion(output_fake, fake_labels)
        errD_fake.backward()

        errD = errD_real + errD_fake
        optimizer_D.step()

        ##############################
        # (2) Update Generator network: maximize log(D(G(z)))
        ##############################
        generator.zero_grad()
        # Since we want G to fool D, we use real_labels here
        output_fake_for_G = discriminator(fake_images) # Pass fake_images again, this time allowing G to update
        errG = criterion(output_fake_for_G, real_labels)
        errG.backward()
        optimizer_G.step()

        if i % 100 == 0:
            print(f'[{epoch+1}/{num_epochs}][{i}/{len(train_loader)}] '
                  f'Loss_D: {errD.item():.4f} Loss_G: {errG.item():.4f}')

    # Save generated images for visualization at end of epoch
    if (epoch + 1) % 10 == 0:
        with torch.no_grad():
            fake_images_vis = generator(fixed_noise).detach().cpu()
            fig, axes = plt.subplots(8, 8, figsize=(8, 8))
            for j, ax in enumerate(axes.flatten()):
                ax.imshow(fake_images_vis[j].squeeze(), cmap='gray')
                ax.axis('off')
            plt.suptitle(f'Epoch {epoch+1} Generated Images')
            plt.show()

print("GAN Training Finished!")

```
This example implements a basic DCGAN (Deep Convolutional GAN) for generating MNIST digits. Notice the use of `nn.ConvTranspose2d` in the Generator for upsampling and `nn.LeakyReLU` in the Discriminator. The training loop shows the adversarial update steps for both networks, including `fake_images.detach()` for the Discriminator's update to ensure only D's parameters are affected.

#### Key concepts
*   **Generative Model:** A type of machine learning model that learns the distribution of training data to generate new, similar data samples.
*   **Generative Adversarial Network (GAN):** A framework for training generative models using an adversarial process between two neural networks: a Generator and a Discriminator.
*   **Generator (G):** A neural network that takes a random noise vector and transforms it into a synthetic data sample, aiming to produce realistic data.
*   **Discriminator (D):** A neural network that takes a data sample (real or fake) and outputs the probability that it is real, aiming to distinguish between real and fake data.
*   **Adversarial Training:** The process where two networks (Generator and Discriminator) compete against each other, leading to improvements in both.
*   **Minimax Game:** The theoretical framework for GAN training, where the Generator tries to minimize the Discriminator's ability to distinguish fakes, and the Discriminator tries to maximize it.
*   **Random Noise Vector (Latent Vector):** The input to the Generator, typically sampled from a simple distribution, which the Generator transforms into a data sample.
*   **Transposed Convolution (Deconvolution):** A type of convolutional layer used in the Generator to upsample feature maps and generate images from a lower-dimensional representation.
*   **Mode Collapse:** A common GAN training problem where the Generator produces only a limited variety of samples, failing to capture the full diversity of the real data distribution.
*   **Training Instability:** The difficulty in balancing the training of the Generator and Discriminator, which can lead to oscillations, non-convergence, or one network overpowering the other.
*   **Binary Cross-Entropy Loss (BCELoss):** A common loss function used for both Generator and Discriminator in GANs, as it's a binary classification problem for the Discriminator.
*   **Gradient Detaching:** Crucial step in GAN training where the gradient flow from the Discriminator is stopped for the Generator's parameters when training the Discriminator, and vice-versa.

#### Hands-on activity
**Experiment with GAN Hyperparameters and Noise Distribution**

Your task is to modify the provided MNIST GAN example to experiment with different hyperparameters and observe their effect on generated image quality and training stability.

```python
import torch
import torch.nn as nn
import torch.optim as optim
from torchvision import datasets, transforms
from torch.utils.data import DataLoader
import matplotlib.pyplot as plt
import numpy as np

# 1. Hyperparameters (TODO: Experiment with these)
latent_dim = 100 # Try 50, 200
img_size = 28
num_channels = 1
batch_size = 64
num_epochs = 50 # Try 100, 200
lr_G = 0.0002 # Try 0.0001, 0.0004
lr_D = 0.0002 # Try 0.0001, 0.0004

# Data loading and preprocessing (MNIST) - same as example
transform = transforms.Compose([
    transforms.ToTensor(),
    transforms.Normalize((0.5,), (0.5,))
])
train_dataset = datasets.MNIST(root='./data', train=True, download=True, transform=transform)
train_loader = DataLoader(train_dataset, batch_size=batch_size, shuffle=True)

# Generator and Discriminator Networks - same as example
class Generator(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, latent_dim, img_size, num_channels):
        super(Generator, self).__init__()
        self.img_size = img_size
        self.num_channels = num_channels
        self.fc = nn.Linear(latent_dim, 128 * (img_size // 4) * (img_size // 4))
        self.main = nn.Sequential(
            nn.BatchNorm2d(128),
            nn.ReLU(True),
            nn.ConvTranspose2d(128, 64, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),
            nn.BatchNorm2d(64),
            nn.ReLU(True),
            nn.ConvTranspose2d(64, num_channels, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),
            nn.Tanh()
        )
    def forward(self, input):
        x = self.fc(input)
        x = x.view(x.size(0), 128, self.img_size // 4, self.img_size // 4)
        return self.main(x)

class Discriminator(nn.Module):
    def __init__(self, img_size, num_channels):
        super(Discriminator, self).__init__()
        self.main = nn.Sequential(
            nn.Conv2d(num_channels, 64, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),
            nn.LeakyReLU(0.2, inplace=True), # TODO: Try nn.ReLU here
            nn.Conv2d(64, 128, kernel_size=4, stride=2, padding=1),

            nn.Conv2d(128, 1, kernel_size=(img_size // 4), stride=1, padding=0),
            nn.Sigmoid()
        )
    def forward(self, input):
        return self.main(input).view(-1, 1)

# Instantiate models, loss, and optimizers
device = torch.device("cuda" if torch.cuda.is_available() else "cpu")
generator = Generator(latent_dim, img_size, num_channels).to(device)
discriminator = Discriminator(img_size, num_channels).to(device)
criterion = nn.BCELoss()
optimizer_G = optim.Adam(generator.parameters(), lr=lr_G, betas=(0.5, 0.999))
optimizer_D = optim.Adam(discriminator.parameters(), lr=lr_D, betas=(0.5, 0.999))
fixed_noise = torch.randn(64, latent_dim, device=device)

print("GAN Hyperparameter Experimentation to be conducted by the learner.")
```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** During GAN training, if the Discriminator's loss consistently approaches zero very quickly, while the Generator's loss remains high and its generated samples do not improve, what is the most likely problem, and what is a common strategy to address it?
    *   A) Mode collapse; increase the `latent_dim` of the Generator.
    *   B) Discriminator is too strong; reduce the Discriminator's learning rate or update it less frequently.
    *   C) Generator is too strong; increase the Generator's learning rate.
    *   D) Vanishing gradients for the Discriminator; switch to a different activation function.

    **Correct Answer:** B) Discriminator is too strong; reduce the Discriminator's learning rate or update it less frequently.
    **Explanation:** If the Discriminator's loss quickly goes to zero, it means it has become too good at distinguishing real from fake images. In this scenario, the Generator receives very poor (or vanishing) gradients, as the Discriminator is always confident in its classification, providing little useful feedback for the Generator to improve. To rebalance the training, one common strategy is to weaken the Discriminator by reducing its learning rate or by updating it fewer times per Generator update.

2.  **Question:** You are training a GAN to generate images of animals. After training for many epochs, you notice that the Generator consistently produces images of only cats, even though the training dataset contains a wide variety of animals (dogs, birds, etc.). What is this phenomenon called, and why does it occur?
    *   A) Exploding gradients; the Discriminator is too weak.
    *   B) Mode collapse; the Generator has found a few types of samples that fool the Discriminator well and ignores the rest of the data distribution.
    *   C) Vanishing gradients; the Generator's learning rate is too high.
    *   D) Overfitting; the Discriminator is not complex enough.

    **Correct Answer:** B) Mode collapse; the Generator has found a few types of samples that fool the Discriminator well and ignores the rest of the data distribution.
    **Explanation:** This scenario perfectly describes **mode collapse**. It happens when the Generator discovers one or a few "modes" (subsets) of the real data distribution that are easy to generate and effectively fool the current Discriminator. Instead of learning the entire diverse distribution of animals, it specializes in generating only cats because it found a local optimum where cats are good enough to pass the Discriminator's checks. The Generator then stops exploring other parts of the data distribution, leading to a lack of diversity in generated samples.

## Module 5: Natural Language Processing (NLP)

This module introduces the fascinating field of Natural Language Processing (NLP), exploring how artificial intelligence can understand, interpret, and generate human language. You will begin with foundational text preprocessing techniques, learn to represent text numerically through various feature engineering methods and advanced word embeddings, and then delve into powerful deep learning architectures like Recurrent Neural Networks (RNNs) and Transformers. The module culminates in an exploration of advanced NLP applications and the critical ethical considerations involved in building language-aware AI systems.

### Chapter 5.1 — Introduction to NLP and Text Preprocessing

#### Learning objectives
*   Define Natural Language Processing (NLP) and identify its core applications in AI.
*   Understand the unique challenges posed by human language data for AI systems.
*   Implement fundamental text preprocessing techniques such as tokenization, lowercasing, and stop word removal using Python libraries.
*   Differentiate between stemming and lemmatization and apply both methods to text data.
*   Recognize the importance of clean and structured text data for effective NLP model training.

#### Detailed lesson content
Welcome to the exciting world of Natural Language Processing, or NLP! At its core, NLP is a subfield of artificial intelligence that focuses on enabling computers to understand, interpret, and generate human language in a valuable and meaningful way. Think about the voice assistants on your phone, the spam filters in your email, or the translation services you use online – these are all powered by NLP. The goal is to bridge the communication gap between humans and computers, allowing machines to process and respond to language as naturally as we do. This capability unlocks a vast array of applications, from sentiment analysis, which gauges the emotional tone of text, to machine translation, which converts text from one language to another, and even sophisticated chatbots that can hold coherent conversations.

However, working with human language presents significant challenges for AI. Unlike structured data found in databases, text is inherently unstructured, ambiguous, and highly contextual. Words can have multiple meanings (polysemy), different words can mean the same thing (synonymy), and the same sequence of words can mean different things depending on intonation or surrounding context. Consider the word "bank" – it could refer to a financial institution or the side of a river. Furthermore, language is constantly evolving, with new words and phrases emerging, and existing ones taking on new connotations. Before any sophisticated AI model can begin to make sense of this complexity, raw text data must undergo a crucial transformation process known as text preprocessing. This step is foundational; without it, models would struggle to identify patterns and relationships within the noise of unrefined language.

The first step in preprocessing is almost always **tokenization**. This is the process of breaking down a continuous stream of text into smaller units called tokens. These tokens are typically words, but they can also be punctuation marks, numbers, or even subword units depending on the tokenizer. For instance, the sentence "I love Cohortia!" might be tokenized into ["I", "love", "Cohortia", "!"]. Tokenization is critical because most NLP tasks operate on a per-token basis. Python libraries like NLTK (Natural Language Toolkit) and spaCy offer robust tokenizers. A common mistake beginners make is to simply split by whitespace, which fails to separate punctuation from words (e.g., "Cohortia!" would remain a single token).

Following tokenization, **lowercasing** is a standard practice. This converts all characters in the text to lowercase, treating "The" and "the" as the same word. This helps reduce the vocabulary size and ensures that the model doesn't treat different capitalizations of the same word as distinct entities, which can be particularly useful for tasks like sentiment analysis or information retrieval. While generally beneficial, there are specific scenarios, such as Named Entity Recognition (NER), where capitalization provides important clues (e.g., "Apple" the company vs. "apple" the fruit), and in such cases, lowercasing might be skipped or applied selectively.

Next, we often perform **stop word removal**. Stop words are common words that carry little semantic meaning and often don't contribute significantly to the overall understanding of a text. Examples include "a," "an," "the," "is," "are," "and," etc. Removing them can reduce the dimensionality of the data, speed up processing, and sometimes improve model performance by focusing on more informative words. NLTK provides a predefined list of stop words for many languages, but it's crucial to remember that what constitutes a "stop word" can be context-dependent. For instance, in a search engine, "the" might be a stop word, but in a legal document analysis, every word could be critical.

Finally, we address the problem of **word variations** through **stemming** and **lemmatization**. English, like many languages, has words with different inflections that share a common root meaning (e.g., "run," "running," "ran").
**Stemming** is a heuristic process that chops off the ends of words to reduce them to a common "stem." For example, "running," "runner," and "runs" might all be reduced to "run." Stemming is typically faster but can be less accurate, sometimes producing non-dictionary words (e.g., "beautiful" might become "beauti"). The Porter Stemmer and Snowball Stemmer are popular algorithms.
**Lemmatization**, on the other hand, is a more sophisticated process that uses a vocabulary and morphological analysis of words to return their base or dictionary form, known as the lemma. So, "running," "ran," and "runs" would all become "run," and "better" would become "good." Lemmatization is generally more accurate than stemming because it ensures the resulting word is a valid word, but it is also computationally more expensive. For most robust NLP applications, lemmatization using libraries like spaCy's lemmatizer is preferred due to its higher accuracy. Choosing between stemming and lemmatization depends on the specific NLP task, the desired level of accuracy, and computational constraints. For tasks where precision in word meaning is paramount, lemmatization is the better choice. For tasks where speed and a general reduction in word forms are sufficient, stemming can be adequate.

By meticulously applying these preprocessing steps, we transform raw, messy human language into a cleaner, more structured format that AI models can effectively learn from. This foundational work is indispensable for building high-performing NLP systems.

#### Key concepts
*   **Natural Language Processing (NLP):** A field of AI focused on enabling computers to understand, interpret, and generate human language.
*   **Text Preprocessing:** The initial stage of cleaning and preparing raw text data for NLP tasks.
*   **Tokenization:** The process of breaking text into smaller units (tokens), typically words or punctuation.
*   **Lowercasing:** Converting all text characters to lowercase to standardize word forms.
*   **Stop Word Removal:** Eliminating common, semantically insignificant words (e.g., "the," "is") to reduce noise and dimensionality.
*   **Stemming:** A heuristic process of reducing words to their root form by chopping off suffixes, often resulting in non-dictionary words.
*   **Lemmatization:** A more sophisticated process of reducing words to their base or dictionary form (lemma) using linguistic rules and vocabulary.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Text Preprocessing Pipeline**

You will implement a basic text preprocessing pipeline using NLTK and spaCy.

```python
import nltk
from nltk.corpus import stopwords
from nltk.stem import PorterStemmer
import spacy

# Download necessary NLTK data (run once)
try:
    nltk.data.find('corpora/stopwords')
except nltk.downloader.DownloadError:
    nltk.download('stopwords')
try:
    nltk.data.find('tokenizers/punkt')
except nltk.downloader.DownloadError:
    nltk.download('punkt')
try:
    nltk.data.find('corpora/wordnet')
except nltk.downloader.DownloadError:
    nltk.download('wordnet')

# Load spaCy English model (run once, if not already downloaded: python -m spacy download en_core_web_sm)
try:
    nlp = spacy.load("en_core_web_sm")
except OSError:
    print("Downloading spaCy model 'en_core_web_sm'...")
    spacy.cli.download("en_core_web_sm")
    nlp = spacy.load("en_core_web_sm")

def preprocess_text(text, use_lemmatization=True):
    # 1. Lowercasing
    text = text.lower()

    # 2. Tokenization
    tokens = nltk.word_tokenize(text)

    # 3. Stop Word Removal
    stop_words = set(stopwords.words('english'))
    filtered_tokens = [word for word in tokens if word.isalpha() and word not in stop_words] # .isalpha() removes punctuation tokens

    # 4. Stemming or Lemmatization
    if use_lemmatization:
        doc = nlp(" ".join(filtered_tokens))
        processed_tokens = [token.lemma_ for token in doc]
    else:
        stemmer = PorterStemmer()
        processed_tokens = [stemmer.stem(word) for word in filtered_tokens]

    return processed_tokens

# Example usage:
sample_text = "Natural Language Processing is an incredibly fascinating field, and Cohortia's diploma program is truly excellent for learning new skills! Students are running towards their goals."

print(f"Original text: {sample_text}\n")

# Preprocessing with Lemmatization
lemmatized_output = preprocess_text(sample_text, use_lemmatization=True)
print(f"Processed (Lemmatized): {lemmatized_output}\n")

# Preprocessing with Stemming
stemmed_output = preprocess_text(sample_text, use_lemmatization=False)
print(f"Processed (Stemmed): {stemmed_output}\n")

# Challenge: Experiment with different texts and observe the differences between stemming and lemmatization.
# Try to find a word where stemming produces a non-dictionary word but lemmatization produces a correct one.
```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** Consider the sentence: "The cats were running quickly through the fields." Apply the following preprocessing steps in order: lowercasing, tokenization, stop word removal (using NLTK's default English stop words), and then lemmatization (assuming "quickly" -> "quick", "cats" -> "cat", "fields" -> "field", "running" -> "run"). What is the final list of processed tokens?
    *   **A) `['cat', 'run', 'quick', 'field']`**
    *   **B) `['cat', 'run', 'quickly', 'field']`**
    *   **C) `['the', 'cat', 'be', 'run', 'quick', 'through', 'the', 'field']`**
    *   **D) `['cat', 'run', 'quickli', 'field']`**

    **Correct Answer:** A) `['cat', 'run', 'quick', 'field']`
    **Explanation:**
    *   **Lowercasing:** "the cats were running quickly through the fields."
    *   **Tokenization:** `['the', 'cats', 'were', 'running', 'quickly', 'through', 'the', 'fields', '.']`
    *   **Stop Word Removal:** NLTK's stop words include 'the', 'were', 'through'. Punctuation '.' is also typically removed. Remaining: `['cats', 'running', 'quickly', 'fields']`
    *   **Lemmatization:** 'cats' -> 'cat', 'running' -> 'run', 'quickly' -> 'quick', 'fields' -> 'field'.
    *   Final list: `['cat', 'run', 'quick', 'field']`

2.  **Question:** Which of the following statements accurately describes a key difference between stemming and lemmatization in NLP?
    *   **A) Stemming is generally slower and more accurate than lemmatization.**
    *   **B) Lemmatization always produces a valid dictionary word, whereas stemming may not.**
    *   **C) Stemming considers the part-of-speech of a word, while lemmatization does not.**
    *   **D) Both stemming and lemmatization use morphological analysis to reduce words to their root form.**

    **Correct Answer:** B) Lemmatization always produces a valid dictionary word, whereas stemming may not.
    **Explanation:** Lemmatization uses linguistic knowledge to return the base form (lemma) which is a valid word in the language. Stemming, being a heuristic process, often just chops off suffixes and can result in non-dictionary "stems." Stemming is typically faster but less accurate, and neither primarily considers part-of-speech in the same way (though lemmatization can benefit from it for better accuracy).

### Chapter 5.2 — Feature Engineering for Text Data

#### Learning objectives
*   Explain the necessity of converting text into numerical representations for machine learning models.
*   Implement the Bag-of-Words (BoW) model to create document-term matrices.
*   Calculate Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF) scores and interpret their meaning.
*   Understand the concept of N-grams and their role in capturing local word order.
*   Identify the limitations of traditional count-based feature engineering methods for text.

#### Detailed lesson content
After meticulously cleaning and preparing our text data through preprocessing, the next crucial step in NLP is to transform this human-readable text into a format that machine learning algorithms can understand and process: numerical vectors. Computers don't "understand" words in the way humans do; they operate on numbers. Therefore, every word, phrase, or document must be converted into a numerical representation, or "feature vector," before it can be fed into a classification, clustering, or regression model. This process is known as **feature engineering for text data**, and it's about extracting meaningful numerical characteristics from the text. The quality of these numerical features directly impacts the performance of any subsequent machine learning model.

One of the simplest and most intuitive methods for text vectorization is the **Bag-of-Words (BoW)** model. Imagine a bag where you throw all the words from a document, ignoring their order but keeping track of how many times each word appears. That's essentially what BoW does. It represents each document as a vector where each dimension corresponds to a unique word in the entire corpus's vocabulary, and the value in that dimension is the frequency of that word in the document.
To construct a BoW model, you first build a vocabulary of all unique words across all documents in your corpus. Then, for each document, you create a vector. If the vocabulary has `V` unique words, each document will be represented by a vector of length `V`. A '1' or the count of a word indicates its presence or frequency, while a '0' indicates its absence.
For example, consider two simple sentences:
1.  "I love Cohortia."
2.  "Cohortia is great."
The combined vocabulary would be: {"I", "love", "Cohortia", "is", "great"}.
Sentence 1's vector (using counts) might be `[1, 1, 1, 0, 0]`.
Sentence 2's vector might be `[0, 0, 1, 1, 1]`.
The `CountVectorizer` from scikit-learn is a powerful tool for implementing BoW. While simple, BoW is effective for many tasks like spam detection or basic topic modeling, but it suffers from a major drawback: it completely disregards word order and context. "Dog bites man" and "Man bites dog" would have identical BoW representations, despite their vastly different meanings.

To address some of the limitations of simple word counts, particularly the issue where very common words (even after stop word removal) can dominate the representation, we turn to **Term Frequency-Inverse Document Frequency (TF-IDF)**. TF-IDF is a statistical measure that evaluates how relevant a word is to a document in a collection of documents (corpus). It's a product of two terms:
1.  **Term Frequency (TF):** This measures how frequently a term appears in a document. It's usually the raw count of a term in a document, or a normalized version (e.g., count divided by total words in the document). A high TF means the word is important within *that specific document*.
2.  **Inverse Document Frequency (IDF):** This measures how rare or common a term is across the entire corpus. It's calculated as the logarithm of the total number of documents divided by the number of documents containing the term. A high IDF means the word is rare across the *entire corpus*, making it potentially more distinctive.
The TF-IDF score for a word in a document is `TF * IDF`. A high TF-IDF score indicates that a word is frequent in a particular document but rare across the entire corpus, suggesting it's highly relevant to that specific document. Conversely, a word that appears frequently in many documents (like "AI" in an AI course corpus) will have a lower IDF, thus a lower TF-IDF score, reducing its importance. This method helps to filter out common words that might not be very informative and highlight words that are unique and specific to a document. `TfidfVectorizer` in scikit-learn handles this efficiently.

While BoW and TF-IDF treat words as independent units, language often relies on sequences of words for meaning. This is where **N-grams** come into play. An N-gram is a contiguous sequence of `n` items from a given sample of text or speech. The "items" can be characters, syllables, or, most commonly in NLP, words.
*   A **unigram** (1-gram) is a single word (e.g., "natural", "language"). This is what BoW uses.
*   A **bigram** (2-gram) is a sequence of two words (e.g., "natural language", "language processing").
*   A **trigram** (3-gram) is a sequence of three words (e.g., "natural language processing").
By including N-grams (especially bigrams and trigrams) in our feature set, we can capture some local word order and context. For instance, the bigram "not good" conveys a very different meaning than "good" alone. N-grams can be incorporated into BoW or TF-IDF models by treating each N-gram as a distinct "token" in the vocabulary. The `CountVectorizer` and `TfidfVectorizer` in scikit-learn allow you to specify an `ngram_range` parameter to generate N-grams. The challenge with N-grams is that they significantly increase the vocabulary size, leading to higher-dimensional and sparser feature vectors, which can be computationally expensive and prone to the "curse of dimensionality."

Despite their utility, traditional count-based methods like BoW and TF-IDF, even with N-grams, have significant **limitations**:
1.  **Sparsity and High Dimensionality:** The vocabulary size can grow enormous, especially with N-grams, leading to very long vectors filled mostly with zeros. This makes models computationally expensive and memory-intensive.
2.  **Lack of Semantic Meaning:** These methods treat words as atomic units without understanding their semantic relationships. "King" and "Queen" are just different words; the models don't know they are related by gender or royalty. They cannot capture synonyms or words with similar meanings.
3.  **No Contextual Understanding:** While N-grams capture local order, they still don't provide a deep contextual understanding of words, especially for words with multiple meanings (polysemy). The word "bank" will have the same representation regardless of whether it's about a river bank or a financial bank.
These limitations paved the way for more advanced representation techniques, particularly word embeddings, which we will explore in the next chapter. Understanding these foundational methods, however, is crucial for appreciating the advancements that followed and for tasks where simplicity and interpretability are prioritized.

#### Key concepts
*   **Feature Engineering for Text:** The process of converting raw text into numerical features suitable for machine learning models.
*   **Bag-of-Words (BoW):** A model that represents a document as an unordered collection of words, typically using word counts or frequencies.
*   **Vocabulary:** The set of all unique words (or tokens) across an entire corpus.
*   **Term Frequency (TF):** The frequency of a specific term within a single document.
*   **Inverse Document Frequency (IDF):** A measure of how important a term is, inversely proportional to the number of documents it appears in across the corpus.
*   **TF-IDF:** A statistical measure reflecting how important a word is to a document in a corpus, calculated as `TF * IDF`.
*   **N-gram:** A contiguous sequence of `n` items (words or characters) from a text.
    *   **Unigram (1-gram):** A single word.
    *   **Bigram (2-gram):** A sequence of two words.
    *   **Trigram (3-gram):** A sequence of three words.
*   **Sparsity:** A characteristic of data where most values are zero, common in BoW/TF-IDF vectors due to large vocabularies.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Implementing BoW and TF-IDF**

You will use scikit-learn to create Bag-of-Words and TF-IDF representations for a small corpus of documents.

```python
from sklearn.feature_extraction.text import CountVectorizer, TfidfVectorizer
import pandas as pd

corpus = [
    "The Cohortia AI diploma is an excellent program.",
    "AI and machine learning are core components of the diploma.",
    "Students learn practical skills in AI and NLP.",
    "The program focuses on real-world AI projects."
]

# --- Part 1: Bag-of-Words (BoW) ---
print("--- Bag-of-Words (BoW) Representation ---")
# Initialize CountVectorizer
# We can specify stop_words='english' to remove common English stop words
# and lowercase=True is default.
count_vectorizer = CountVectorizer(stop_words='english')

# Fit and transform the corpus
bow_matrix = count_vectorizer.fit_transform(corpus)

# Get feature names (words in the vocabulary)
feature_names = count_vectorizer.get_feature_names_out()

# Convert to DataFrame for better visualization
bow_df = pd.DataFrame(bow_matrix.toarray(), columns=feature_names)
print("BoW Matrix:")
print(bow_df)
print(f"\nVocabulary size (BoW): {len(feature_names)}")

# --- Part 2: TF-IDF Representation ---
print("\n--- TF-IDF Representation ---")
# Initialize TfidfVectorizer
# It also handles tokenization, lowercasing, and stop word removal by default
tfidf_vectorizer = TfidfVectorizer(stop_words='english')

# Fit and transform the corpus
tfidf_matrix = tfidf_vectorizer.fit_transform(corpus)

# Get feature names (words in the vocabulary)
tfidf_feature_names = tfidf_vectorizer.get_feature_names_out()

# Convert to DataFrame for better visualization
tfidf_df = pd.DataFrame(tfidf_matrix.toarray(), columns=tfidf_feature_names)
print("TF-IDF Matrix:")
print(tfidf_df)
print(f"\nVocabulary size (TF-IDF): {len(tfidf_feature_names)}")

# --- Part 3: Experiment with N-grams (Bigrams) ---
print("\n--- BoW with Bigrams ---")
ngram_vectorizer = CountVectorizer(stop_words='english', ngram_range=(1, 2)) # Include unigrams and bigrams
ngram_matrix = ngram_vectorizer.fit_transform(corpus)
ngram_feature_names = ngram_vectorizer.get_feature_names_out()
ngram_df = pd.DataFrame(ngram_matrix.toarray(), columns=ngram_feature_names)
print("BoW Matrix with Bigrams:")
print(ngram_df)
print(f"\nVocabulary size (BoW + Bigrams): {len(ngram_feature_names)}")

# Challenge:
# 1. Add another sentence to the corpus and re-run the code. Observe how the matrices change.
# 2. Try to interpret why certain words have higher TF-IDF scores than others.
# 3. Modify the ngram_range to (2,2) to only include bigrams and see the difference.
```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You are building a spam filter and have two emails. Email A: "Buy now, great deal!" Email B: "Great deal on Cohortia courses." Using a Bag-of-Words model with a vocabulary of `{"buy", "now", "great", "deal", "on", "cohortia", "courses"}`, what would be the numerical representation (vector) for Email B? Assume binary presence (1 for present, 0 for absent).
    *   **A) `[0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1]`**
    *   **B) `[1, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0]`**
    *   **C) `[0, 0, 1, 1, 0, 0, 0]`**
    *   **D) `[0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 0, 0]`**

    **Correct Answer:** A) `[0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1]`
    **Explanation:**
    *   The vocabulary is ordered: "buy", "now", "great", "deal", "on", "cohortia", "courses".
    *   Email B: "Great deal on Cohortia courses."
    *   "buy": 0
    *   "now": 0
    *   "great": 1
    *   "deal": 1
    *   "on": 1
    *   "cohortia": 1
    *   "courses": 1
    *   Resulting vector: `[0, 0, 1, 1, 1, 1, 1]`

2.  **Question:** In a corpus of 100 documents, the word "AI" appears in 90 documents, and the word "Transformer" appears in 5 documents. If both words appear 5 times in a specific document, which word is likely to have a higher TF-IDF score for that document, and why?
    *   **A) "AI", because it appears more frequently in the corpus.**
    *   **B) "Transformer", because it is rarer across the corpus, leading to a higher IDF.**
    *   **C) Both will have the same TF-IDF score because their Term Frequency (TF) is the same in the document.**
    *   **D) Neither, as TF-IDF only considers the total count, not document frequency.**

    **Correct Answer:** B) "Transformer", because it is rarer across the corpus, leading to a higher IDF.
    **Explanation:** TF-IDF is the product of Term Frequency (TF) and Inverse Document Frequency (IDF).
    *   **TF:** Both words have the same TF (5) in the specific document.
    *   **IDF for "AI":** `log(100 / 90)` which is a small positive number.
    *   **IDF for "Transformer":** `log(100 / 5)` which is `log(20)`, a much larger positive number.
    Since the IDF for "Transformer" is significantly higher, its TF-IDF score will be higher, indicating it's more distinctive to documents it appears in compared to "AI," which is very common.

Then, transition to a live coding session in a Jupyter Notebook, demonstrating `CountVectorizer` and `TfidfVectorizer` from scikit-learn on a small corpus of 4-5 sentences related to AI and machine learning. ### Chapter 5.3 — Word Embeddings: Word2Vec and GloVe

#### Learning objectives
*   Explain the limitations of count-based text representations (BoW, TF-IDF) and the motivation for word embeddings.
*   Describe the core idea behind distributed representations and how they capture semantic relationships.
*   Understand the architectures and training mechanisms of Word2Vec (Skip-gram and CBOW).
*   Grasp the fundamental principles of GloVe (Global Vectors for Word Representation) and its hybrid approach.
*   Utilize pre-trained word embeddings in a practical NLP scenario and discuss their benefits.

#### Detailed lesson content
While Bag-of-Words and TF-IDF models provided a foundational way to convert text into numbers, they suffered from significant drawbacks, primarily their inability to capture the semantic meaning or relationships between words. In these models, "king" and "queen" were just two distinct, orthogonal dimensions in a high-dimensional space, with no inherent connection. The vectors were sparse, high-dimensional, and lacked any real understanding of synonyms, antonyms, or contextual nuances. This "curse of dimensionality" and the semantic gap severely limited the performance of machine learning models on complex NLP tasks. The need for a richer, more meaningful representation of words became apparent, leading to the development of **word embeddings**.

Word embeddings are dense, low-dimensional vector representations of words that capture their semantic and syntactic relationships. The core idea is that words that appear in similar contexts tend to have similar meanings. This is known as the **distributional hypothesis**. Instead of a sparse vector where each dimension is a word, an embedding represents a word as a continuous vector of real numbers (e.g., 50, 100, 300 dimensions). In this dense vector space, words with similar meanings are located closer to each other. For example, the vector for "king" might be very close to the vector for "queen" or "prince." Even more remarkably, these embeddings can capture analogies: the vector difference between "king" and "man" might be similar to the vector difference between "queen" and "woman" (i.e., `vector("king") - vector("man") + vector("woman") ≈ vector("queen")`). This property makes embeddings incredibly powerful for tasks requiring nuanced understanding of language.

One of the most influential early models for generating word embeddings is **Word2Vec**, introduced by Google in 2013. Word2Vec is not a deep neural network in the traditional sense, but rather a shallow, two-layer neural network trained to reconstruct linguistic contexts of words. It comes in two main architectures:
1.  **Skip-gram:** This model tries to predict the surrounding context words given a target word. For example, if the target word is "king," Skip-gram might try to predict "the," "royal," "throne," "man," etc., within a certain window. It's particularly good at capturing rare words.
2.  **Continuous Bag-of-Words (CBOW):** This model does the opposite; it predicts the target word given its surrounding context words. For example, given "the ___ is royal," CBOW might predict "king." CBOW is generally faster to train and performs well for more frequent words.
Both architectures learn word embeddings by training on a large corpus of text. The training objective is to maximize the probability of context words given a target word (Skip-gram) or vice versa (CBOW). The magic happens in the hidden layer, where the weights connecting the input layer to the hidden layer (or the hidden layer to the output layer) become the learned word embeddings. These embeddings are then extracted and can be used as features for other NLP tasks. A common mistake is to think Word2Vec is a classification model; it's primarily an embedding learning technique.

Another prominent word embedding model is **GloVe (Global Vectors for Word Representation)**, developed by Stanford. GloVe takes a slightly different approach, aiming to combine the advantages of both global matrix factorization methods (like Latent Semantic Analysis) and local context window methods (like Word2Vec). Instead of predicting context words directly, GloVe trains on global word-word co-occurrence statistics from the entire corpus. It constructs a large co-occurrence matrix, where each entry `X_ij` represents how many times word `j` appears in the context of word `i`. GloVe then tries to learn word vectors such that their dot product is related to the logarithm of their co-occurrence probability. This allows GloVe to capture both local context (through the co-occurrence window) and global statistics (through the entire corpus matrix). The result is a set of word embeddings that often perform comparably to or better than Word2Vec, especially on tasks that benefit from global statistical information.

The immense benefit of word embeddings is that they can be **pre-trained** on massive text corpora (like Wikipedia, Common Crawl, or Google News) and then directly used or fine-tuned for specific downstream NLP tasks. This significantly reduces the need for large, labeled datasets for every new task and leverages the vast amount of general linguistic knowledge encoded in these pre-trained embeddings. For instance, if you're building a sentiment analysis model for movie reviews, you don't need to train embeddings from scratch. You can download pre-trained Word2Vec or GloVe embeddings, which already understand that "amazing" and "fantastic" are similar, and use them to represent the words in your reviews. This transfer learning approach is a cornerstone of modern NLP.

To use pre-trained embeddings, you typically load a file (e.g., a `.vec` or `.txt` file containing words and their corresponding vectors) into memory. When processing a new document, you look up the embedding for each word. If a word is not in the pre-trained vocabulary, you might represent it with a zero vector or a random vector. Libraries like Gensim make working with Word2Vec models straightforward, while spaCy integrates pre-trained word vectors directly into its language models.
Safety note: When using pre-trained embeddings, it's crucial to be aware of potential biases. If the training data contains societal biases (e.g., "doctor" is more frequently associated with "he" than "she"), these biases will be encoded in the embeddings. This can lead to unfair or discriminatory outcomes in downstream applications, making ethical considerations paramount when deploying NLP systems built on such embeddings. As AI practitioners, we must be vigilant in identifying and mitigating these biases.

#### Key concepts
*   **Word Embeddings:** Dense, low-dimensional vector representations of words that capture semantic and syntactic relationships.
*   **Distributional Hypothesis:** The idea that words appearing in similar contexts tend to have similar meanings.
*   **Word2Vec:** A family of models (Skip-gram and CBOW) for learning word embeddings by predicting context words from target words or vice versa.
    *   **Skip-gram:** Predicts context words given a target word. Good for rare words.
    *   **Continuous Bag-of-Words (CBOW):** Predicts a target word given its context words. Faster for frequent words.
*   **GloVe (Global Vectors for Word Representation):** An embedding model that leverages global word-word co-occurrence statistics from a corpus.
*   **Pre-trained Embeddings:** Word embeddings learned from massive text corpora (e.g., Wikipedia) that can be reused for various NLP tasks.
*   **Semantic Relationship:** The connection of meaning between words (e.g., synonyms, antonyms, analogies).
*   **Bias in Embeddings:** The reflection of societal biases present in the training data within the learned word vectors, potentially leading to unfair AI outcomes.

#### Hands-on activity
**Activity: Exploring Pre-trained Word Embeddings**

You will load and explore a pre-trained GloVe embedding model to understand word similarity and analogies. We'll use the `gensim` library for convenience, which can load GloVe embeddings.

```python
import gensim.downloader as api
from scipy.spatial.distance import cosine
import numpy as np

# Download a small pre-trained GloVe model (glove-wiki-gigaword-50)
# This might take a few minutes the first time you run it.
print("Downloading pre-trained GloVe model (glove-wiki-gigaword-50)...")
try:
    glove_model = api.load("glove-wiki-gigaword-50")
    print("GloVe model loaded successfully.")
except Exception as e:
    print(f"Error loading model: {e}")
    print("Please ensure you have an active internet connection and sufficient disk space.")
    print("You might need to install gensim: pip install gensim")
    exit()

# --- Part 1: Word Similarity ---
print("\n--- Word Similarity ---")
word1 = "king"
word2 = "queen"
word3 = "man"
word4 = "woman"
word5 = "computer"
word6 = "laptop"
word7 = "apple"
word8 = "orange"

# Find similarity between words
print(f"Similarity between '{word1}' and '{word2}': {glove_model.similarity(word1, word2):.4f}")
print(f"Similarity between '{word5}' and '{word6}': {glove_model.similarity(word5, word6):.4f}")
print(f"Similarity between '{word7}' and '{word8}': {glove_model.similarity(word7, word8):.4f}")
print(f"Similarity between '{word1}' and '{word5}': {glove_model.similarity(word1, word5):.4f}") # Expect low similarity

# Find most similar words
print(f"\nWords most similar to '{word1}':")
for word, score in glove_model.most_similar(word1, topn=5):
    print(f"  {word}: {score:.4f}")

print(f"\nWords most similar to '{word7}':")
for word, score in glove_model.most_similar(word7, topn=5):
    print(f"  {word}: {score:.4f}")

# --- Part 2: Word Analogies (A is to B as C is to ?) ---
print("\n--- Word Analogies ---")
# Example: king - man + woman = queen
analogy_result = glove_model.most_similar(positive=['woman', 'king'], negative=['man'], topn=1)
print(f"King - Man + Woman = {analogy_result[0][0]} (score: {analogy_result[0][1]:.4f})")

# Example: Paris - France + Italy = Rome
analogy_result_city = glove_model.most_similar(positive=['italy', 'paris'], negative=['france'], topn=1)
print(f"Paris - France + Italy = {analogy_result_city[0][0]} (score: {analogy_result_city[0][1]:.4f})")

# 3. Explore a larger GloVe model if your system allows (e.g., "glove-wiki-gigaword-100" for 100 dimensions).
```

#### Assessment idea
1.  **Question:** You observe that in a pre-trained word embedding space, the vector for "doctor" is closer to "he" than "she," and "nurse" is closer to "she" than "he." What does this phenomenon indicate, and why is it a concern for AI development?
    *   **A) It indicates that the embedding model is highly accurate in capturing semantic relationships, which is always beneficial.**
    *   **B) It suggests that the training data for the embeddings contained societal gender biases, which can lead to unfair or discriminatory outcomes in downstream NLP applications.**
    *   **C) It means the model has learned to correctly classify the gender of medical professionals, which is a desirable feature.**
    *   **D) This is a normal and harmless artifact of word embedding training and has no practical implications.**

    **Correct Answer:** B) It suggests that the training data for the embeddings contained societal gender biases, which can lead to unfair or discriminatory outcomes in downstream NLP applications.
    **Explanation:** Word embeddings learn from the statistical patterns in large text corpora. If the training data reflects real-world societal biases (e.g., more mentions of male doctors and female nurses), these biases will be encoded into the vector space. This is a significant concern because any AI system using these biased embeddings for tasks like job application screening or medical diagnosis could perpetuate or amplify these biases, leading to unfair or discriminatory decisions.

2.  **Question:** Which of the following statements best describes the primary advantage of word embeddings (like Word2Vec or GloVe) over traditional count-based methods (like Bag-of-Words or TF-IDF)?
    *   **A) Word embeddings are always faster to compute for large corpora.**
    *   **B) Word embeddings create sparse, high-dimensional representations, which are easier for models to process.**
    *   **C) Word embeddings capture semantic relationships and contextual meaning between words, leading to richer representations.**
    *   **D) Word embeddings eliminate the need for any text preprocessing steps.**

    **Correct Answer:** C) Word embeddings capture semantic relationships and contextual meaning between words, leading to richer representations.
    **Explanation:** The key innovation of word embeddings is their ability to represent words in a dense, continuous vector space where semantic and syntactic relationships are preserved. Words with similar meanings are close together, and analogies can be captured. This is a significant improvement over count-based methods which treat words as independent units and fail to capture such relationships, resulting in sparse and semantically poor representations. While embeddings can be computationally intensive to train, they offer a qualitative leap in representing language meaning. Preprocessing is still necessary.
